People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I guess… people keep saying I’m hard to follow lately. I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.

Yeah… it is. I mean, I want to say it’s just communication style, because artists are allowed to be a little nonlinear, right? Like sometimes the best ideas come from the side door. But no, it’s been costing me.

Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.

Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.

Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.

It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. Not always, but sometimes it feels way bigger than what they actually said. Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily sidetracked by things around you or by your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.\n\nAnd then it can flip over something small. Like a collaborator makes one comment—nothing cruel, just a “hmm, I don’t know”—and I’ll snap, or get sharp, like they’ve attacked the entire structure of what I’m trying to do. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… people keep saying I’m hard to follow lately. Like I’ll be trying to write an artist statement or notes for a client, or I’m in a meeting, and I start explaining one thing—textures, the surface of a piece—and then somehow I’m talking about the budget, and then what the colors secretly mean, and everyone’s just looking at me like I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining your thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I want to say it’s just communication style, because artists are allowed to be a little nonlinear, right? Like sometimes the best ideas come from the side door. One exhibition actually got postponed because the written material I gave them wasn’t usable. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.

Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not important.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how consistent it’s been. It comes and goes, or I notice it after the fact more than while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day” clearly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.

So I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… people keep saying I’m hard to follow lately. Like I’ll be trying to write an artist statement or notes for a client, or I’m in a meeting, and I start explaining one thing—textures, the surface of a piece—and then somehow I’m talking about the budget, and then what the colors secretly mean, and everyone’s just looking at me like I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining your thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I want to say it’s just communication style, because artists are allowed to be a little nonlinear, right? Like sometimes the best ideas come from the side door. But no, it’s been costing me.\n\nI’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense, and in meetings I can tell collaborators are confused—like I’ll be explaining the concept and suddenly I’m somewhere else entirely. One exhibition actually got postponed because the written material I gave them wasn’t usable. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. Not always, but sometimes it feels way bigger than what they actually said. Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily sidetracked by things around you or by your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.\n\nAnd then it can flip over something small. Like a collaborator makes one comment—nothing cruel, just a “hmm, I don’t know”—and I’ll snap, or get sharp, like they’ve attacked the entire structure of what I’m trying to do. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not important.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how consistent it’s been. It comes and goes, or I notice it after the fact more than while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day” clearly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. I’ll think, “Oh, I’ve been off,” but then I get distracted reorganizing brushes or answering emails, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mood swings are related to any specific events or triggers, or do they seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.\n\nBut then, if a collaborator makes one small comment—like, not even a cruel comment, just “maybe this part needs to be simpler”—I can snap really sharply. And afterward it seems way bigger than the situation called for.\n\nSo I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I mean… my thoughts do race sometimes. It’s like I’ll start with one idea for a piece, and before I can even finish that thought, there are images and titles and colors and bits of grant language all piling on top of each other. It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not important.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how consistent it’s been. It comes and goes, or I notice it after the fact more than while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day” clearly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. I’ll think, “Oh, I’ve been off,” but then I get distracted reorganizing brushes or answering emails, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mood swings are related to any specific events or triggers, or do they seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.\n\nBut then, if a collaborator makes one small comment—like, not even a cruel comment, just “maybe this part needs to be simpler”—I can snap really sharply. And afterward it seems way bigger than the situation called for.\n\nSo I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy and your thoughts were racing, and this lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… my thoughts do race sometimes. It’s like I’ll start with one idea for a piece, and before I can even finish that thought, there are images and titles and colors and bits of grant language all piling on top of each other. And then I’ll remember a conversation with someone and that gets folded in too, like it all connects somehow.\n\nThe “at least a week” part is harder for me to be exact about. I don’t always notice the time passing in a clean way when it’s happening. But the racing thoughts part, yes, definitely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Four days… I think so, maybe. I do get these stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole shape of a project at once and every idea feels kind of brilliant. I’ll be in the studio and it feels like I could just keep going, like I’m unstoppable.

But then it can flip into irritation really fast. Like a collaborator will say one small thing—nothing dramatic, just a comment—and I snap back like they’ve insulted the entire piece, or me, or… I don’t know. Later I can see it was bigger than the situation. The exact number of days is hard for me to pin down, though.

I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining your thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. One exhibition actually got postponed because the written material I gave them wasn’t usable. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. Not always, but sometimes it feels way bigger than what they actually said. Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily sidetracked by things around you or by your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.\n\nAnd then it can flip over something small. Like a collaborator makes one comment—nothing cruel, just a “hmm, I don’t know”—and I’ll snap, or get sharp, like they’ve attacked the entire structure of what I’m trying to do. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I don’t always notice the time passing in a clean way when it’s happening. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… not so much like, I bought a car or something, but it has caused problems. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I got so caught in the idea of what the work was “supposed” to become, and then the practical version just… didn’t happen.

And meetings got weird. I’d come in with pages of notes, but they were all branching and connected in my head, and I could see it, but the collaborators couldn’t follow me. I remember one person just looking at the paper like it was a map of a city they’d never been to.

There was an exhibition that had to be postponed because my notes and proposal stopped making enough sense to actually use. So… yeah. Consequences, definitely. Mostly work and relationships with collaborators, not like jail or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… people keep saying I’m hard to follow lately. Like I’ll be trying to write an artist statement or notes for a client, or I’m in a meeting, and I start explaining one thing—textures, the surface of a piece—and then somehow I’m talking about the budget, and then what the colors secretly mean, and everyone’s just looking at me like I’ve lost the thread. I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining your thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I want to say it’s just communication style, because artists are allowed to be a little nonlinear, right? Like sometimes the best ideas come from the side door. But no, it’s been costing me.\n\nI’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense, and in meetings I can tell collaborators are confused—like I’ll be explaining the concept and suddenly I’m somewhere else entirely. One exhibition actually got postponed because the written material I gave them wasn’t usable. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. Not always, but sometimes it feels way bigger than what they actually said. Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily sidetracked by things around you or by your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.\n\nAnd then it can flip over something small. Like a collaborator makes one comment—nothing cruel, just a “hmm, I don’t know”—and I’ll snap, or get sharp, like they’ve attacked the entire structure of what I’m trying to do. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not important.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how consistent it’s been. It comes and goes, or I notice it after the fact more than while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day” clearly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. I’ll think, “Oh, I’ve been off,” but then I get distracted reorganizing brushes or answering emails, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mood swings are related to any specific events or triggers, or do they seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. I’ll think, “Oh, I’ve been off,” but then I get distracted reorganizing brushes or answering emails, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mood swings are related to any specific events or triggers, or do they seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.\n\nBut then, if a collaborator makes one small comment—like, not even a cruel comment, just “maybe this part needs to be simpler”—I can snap really sharply. And afterward it seems way bigger than the situation called for.\n\nSo I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy and your thoughts were racing, and this lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… my thoughts do race sometimes. It’s like I’ll start with one idea for a piece, and before I can even finish that thought, there are images and titles and colors and bits of grant language all piling on top of each other. And then I’ll remember a conversation with someone and that gets folded in too, like it all connects somehow.\n\nThe “at least a week” part is harder for me to be exact about. I don’t always notice the time passing in a clean way when it’s happening. But the racing thoughts part, yes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy that lasted for at least four consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Four days… I think so, maybe. I do get these stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole shape of a project at once and every idea feels kind of brilliant. Seven days exactly is hard for me to say. I don’t keep track well when I’m in it. But I do get stretches where I feel really lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable, and every idea feels brilliant—like the whole project is suddenly obvious.

There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.\n\nBut then, if a collaborator makes one small comment—like, not even a cruel comment, just “maybe this part needs to be simpler”—I can snap really sharply. And afterward it seems way bigger than the situation called for.\n\nSo I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy and your thoughts were racing, and this lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… my thoughts do race sometimes. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can swear it was seven days with absolutely no breaks. When I’m in it, I’m not exactly counting days. But I do get these stretches where I feel… lit up, like everything is connecting and I’m almost unstoppable, like every idea is brilliant.

And then it can turn sharp. Like a collaborator says one small thing—sometimes not even critical, just a comment—and I snap back like it means way more than it does. Later I’m like, why did I react that strongly? So yes to the kind of mood, but the exact seven-days-no-breaks part is harder for me to be certain about.

I don’t always notice it until they say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” So… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining your thoughts affecting your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I want to say it’s just communication style, because artists are allowed to be a little nonlinear, right? Like sometimes the best ideas come from the side door. But no, it’s been costing me.\n\nI’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense, and in meetings I can tell collaborators are confused—like I’ll be explaining the concept and suddenly I’m somewhere else entirely. One exhibition actually got postponed because the written material I gave them wasn’t usable. That was… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or mood that seem to coincide with these difficulties in explaining your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s like I’ll have these stretches where I feel really lit up, like everything is connecting and every idea is brilliant, and I can just keep going. And then when I’m talking, I guess I assume everyone can see the same pattern I’m seeing.\n\nBut then someone will make some small comment—like a collaborator saying, “I’m not sure that fits”—and I’ll snap. Not always, but sometimes it feels way bigger than what they actually said. Like suddenly I’m irritated and defensive, and then I’m off explaining ten different reasons they’re missing the point… which probably doesn’t help the whole “hard to follow” thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily sidetracked by things around you or by your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to write a proposal and I’ll mean to just do one paragraph, but then the light in the studio is buzzing, or there’s this shadow moving on the wall, and suddenly I’m thinking about how that shape would work in a piece.\n\nOr I’ll open an old notebook to check one line and that line feels important, like it’s pointing somewhere else, and then I’m off writing something completely different. It’s not even that I choose to switch tasks exactly—it just pulls me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “extreme” sounds dramatic, but it does feel like I swing pretty hard sometimes. I’ll have these stretches where I feel completely lit up, like I’m almost unstoppable and every idea I have is brilliant, like the whole room is finally tuned to the right frequency.\n\nAnd then it can flip over something small. Like a collaborator makes one comment—nothing cruel, just a “hmm, I don’t know”—and I’ll snap, or get sharp, like they’ve attacked the entire structure of what I’m trying to do. And then later I’m like… why did that feel so huge?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that aren't there when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s mostly when I’m alone in the studio, when it’s really quiet—well, not quiet quiet, because old buildings always make noises, and mine has these shelves that creak like they’re settling or breathing or something.\n\nBut there are times I hear a woman’s voice, like she’s behind the shelves, just quietly commenting. Not shouting or anything. It feels real enough that I’ll stop what I’m doing and check back there. And sometimes I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but there’s nothing there when I look.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your thoughts and moods?\n\nPatient: I think… about a little over a year. Like, maybe one year and a couple months? It really started after I left community curation and went fully into doing independent installation work.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the shift, you know—different schedule, more time alone, no one else setting the rhythm. And independent work sounds freeing, but it’s also like suddenly every wall and deadline and invoice is talking at once. But yeah, the thoughts and moods being harder to manage… that’s been around 14 months, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these difficulties with your thoughts and moods go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are calmer patches where I can sort of get through the day and not feel like everything is turned up to maximum.\n\nBut looking back, the wired, irritable, overproductive times have taken up more of the year than the quieter stretches. It kind of blurs together, because I’ll think I’m “fine” if I’m getting a lot done, like making lists and moving materials around and answering emails at weird hours—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess the short answer is: it doesn’t really go away completely. It just gets less intense sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like I can see the whole piece finished before I’ve even started it, and every idea seems connected and kind of brilliant. It feels good at first, like I’m finally moving at the right speed.\n\nBut then it can flip really fast. Like if a collaborator makes one small comment—something normal, probably—I’ll snap, and it feels way bigger than the situation. I’ll hear myself being sharp and part of me knows it’s too much, but in the moment it feels completely justified.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a period of time where you were consistently sad or had no interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, sad, yes, but I have a hard time telling if it’s that kind of sad you mean or just being disappointed when a project doesn’t land the way I imagined.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll stand in my studio and look at all the materials and feel nothing for them, which is weird because usually I love that part—the textures, arranging things, even the smell of wood glue, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not important.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure how consistent it’s been. It comes and goes, or I notice it after the fact more than while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt consistently sad or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day” clearly. Like, I know there are times I feel kind of hollow or flat, but then I’ll get distracted by something in the studio or a message from someone and it changes, or I think it changes.\n\nSometimes I only realize later that I’d been moving through the whole day like everything was behind glass. But I don’t know if that counts as consistently sad or empty, exactly. I’m sorry, I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t track it very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks… I don’t know. I’m not trying to be vague, it’s just hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nI can think of times where everything felt muted for a while, like I was kind of dragging myself through the day, but whether it was most of the day every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t track it that cleanly. I’ll think, “Oh, I’ve been off,” but then I get distracted reorganizing brushes or answering emails, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mood swings are related to any specific events or triggers, or do they seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: Some of it feels tied to things, but not always in a neat way. Like, I’ll have these stretches where I feel kind of lit up—like every idea is connected and brilliant and I can do anything with it. I’ll be in the studio pulling out materials and it feels almost… unstoppable, like everything has meaning.\n\nBut then, if a collaborator makes one small comment—like, not even a cruel comment, just “maybe this part needs to be simpler”—I can snap really sharply. And afterward it seems way bigger than the situation called for.\n\nSo I guess there are triggers sometimes, but the level of my reaction doesn’t always match the trigger, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy and your thoughts were racing, and this lasted for at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… my thoughts do race sometimes. It’s like I’ll start with one idea for a piece, and before I can even finish that thought, there are images and titles and colors and bits of grant language all piling on top of each other. And then I’ll remember a conversation with someone and that gets folded in too, like it all connects somehow.\n\nThe “at least a week” part is harder for me to be exact about. I don’t always notice the time passing in a clean way when it’s happening. But the racing thoughts part, yes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy that lasted for at least four consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Four days… I think so, maybe.
